Contraindications And Precautions
  Headline
  Contraindications/Warnings/Precautions
  General Information
  Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine combinations are contraindicated in patients with known belladonna alkaloids hypersensitivity or barbiturate hypersensitivity because they may develop an allergic or other adverse reaction. Barbiturates can cause severe and potentially fatal reactions that are preceded by skin eruptions, and may present as various organ system problems, including cardiac, hematologic, liver, renal, and skin disorders. A history of hypersensitivity reactions should be obtained for the patient and the immediate family members. Hypersensitivity reactions to phenobarbital have been reported in patients who previously experienced hydantoin hypersensitivity or carbamazepine hypersensitivity; estimates of such cross-sensitivity vary (see Phenobarbital monograph). The immediate-release tablets contain lactose. Patients with lactase deficiency should use appropriate precautions with use. In addition, atropine; hyoscyamine; phenobarbital; scopolamine is contraindicated in patients who experience excitement or restlessness when exposed to phenobarbital.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be prescribed with caution to patients with known substance abuse because of the potential for psychological and/or physical dependence to phenobarbital. Because of the potential for drug dependence and tolerance after prolonged use, avoid abrupt discontinuation. Because of the potential for toxicity, this drug combination should not be taken in amounts above prescription limits.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine may cause blurred vision, drowsiness, or dizziness, especially with initial use. Patients should use caution when driving or operating machinery until they are aware of the effects of the drug. Ethanol intoxication can magnify sedative effects and is best avoided. Effects of this medication may be additive to other anticholinergic medications, and duplicate therapy should be avoided.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be used only with extreme caution in patients with mental status changes, major depression or suicidal ideation because the CNS-depressant effects of phenobarbital can exacerbate these conditions.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be used with caution in the elderly. The elderly are generally more sensitive to the anticholinergics and the sedative effects of phenobarbital. The elderly are also more likely to have medical conditions aggravated by the use of this drug combination, or to experience paradoxical CNS stimulation or from phenobarbital or antimuscarinic drugs.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be used with caution in patients with known cardiac disease. Antimuscarinics may alter the heart rate; the predominant clinical effect is tachycardia. Thus antimuscarinics should be used cautiously in patients with cardiac arrhythmias, congestive heart failure, coronary artery disease or other cardiac instability where an increase in heart rate could be detrimental. Antimuscarinics are relatively contraindicated in some patients with acute myocardial infarction because the drug can potentiate arrhythmias. In addition, the increase in heart rate caused by atropine increases the oxygen demand on the heart and can exacerbate myocardial ischemia. Increased heart rate is undesirable in patients with hyperthyroidism (thyrotoxicosis) or cardiovascular instability in acute hemorrhage (bleeding). Antimuscarinics should be used with caution in patients with mitral stenosis since tachycardia could exacerbate the clinical symptoms of this condition. Antimuscarinics should be used with caution in patients with hypertension since they have some actions on the heart and can exacerbate this condition.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be used cautiously in those with pulmonary disease. The use of antimuscarinics can dry and thicken bronchial secretions in the respiratory tract thereby aggravating respiratory infection, or asthma or chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) in some patients; use with caution in patients with chronic pulmonary disease. Because phenobarbital can cause respiratory depression, it should be used cautiously in patients with any pulmonary disease state causing respiratory depression or insufficiency.
 
Antimuscarinics and barbiturates should be used cautiously in patients with renal impairment. Metabolites and unchanged drug are excreted in the kidneys. Renal failure can lead to phenobarbital toxicity because >= 25% of the drug is excreted unchanged in the urine. Further, antimuscarinics can cause urinary retention. Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine is therefore contraindicated in patients with bladder obstruction, prostatic hypertrophy, or other urinary tract obstruction because urinary retention may be aggravated. Urinary retention may also be aggravated in patients with autonomic neuropathy.
 
Antimuscarinics and barbiturates should be used cautiously in patients with hepatic disease. The liver metabolizes these drugs.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should not be used in patients with acute intermittent porphyria. Phenobarbital can trigger symptoms of this disease. Barbiturates can stimulate the activity of the enzyme proximal to the deficient enzyme, causing a buildup of porphyrin precursors.The phenobarbital in Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine may decrease the effect of oral coumarin anticoagulant therapy (e.g., warfarin, see Drug Interactions) and necessitate upward dosage adjustment of the anticoagulant for optimal effect. Conversely, when the drug is discontinued, the dose of the anticoagulant may have to be decreased.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be avoided in patients with myasthenia gravis because the anticholinergic competes with the small amount of acetylcholine that has potential to act in the body in these patients. This drug combination should be similarly avoided or used with extreme caution in patients with autonomic neuropathy.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be administered with caution to many patients with GI disease. Antimuscarinics are contraindicated in GI obstruction (e.g., achalasia, pyloric stenosis) because they further decrease GI motility and can cause paralytic ileus. Likewise, antimuscarinics are contraindicated in severe ulcerative colitis especially if associated with toxic megacolon, ileus, and intestinal atony because they decrease GI motility and can exacerbate these conditions. Use extreme caution in persons with suspected or known GI infection such as infectious diarrhea (e.g., pseudomembranous colitis) because the antimuscarinics can decrease GI motility and may decrease elimination of the bacteria or toxin from the body and thus prolong the infection. Also, antimuscarinics should be used with caution in patients with diarrhea that may be an early sign of incomplete GI obstruction, especially in patients with ileostomy or colostomy. This drug should also be used cautiously in patients with gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD) or hiatal hernia associated with reflux esophagitis. The antimuscarinics may decrease gastric motility and relax the lower esophageal sphincter, promoting gastric retention and reflux. Although antimuscarinics have been used as adjuncts to the treatment of peptic ulcer disease, there are no conclusive data that these drugs aid in healing, decrease rate of recurrence, or prevent the complications of peptic ulcer. In patients with gastric ulcer, antimuscarinics may delay gastric emptying and promote antral stasis.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine should be used with caution in patients with glaucoma. Antimuscarinics should not be used in closed-angle glaucoma because they can induce cycloplegia and mydriasis, which would increase intraocular pressure. Antimuscarinics should be avoided in patients with synechia (iris adhesion to the cornea or the capsule of the crystalline lens). The anticholinergic effects may also make the eyes dry; this can cause an increased lens awareness, or blurred vision for wearers of contact lenses. The use of lubricating drops may be necessary, or in severe cases discontinued use of contact lenses while taking this drug combination.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine can suppress sweat gland activity causing increases in body temperature. Antimuscarinics should be prescribed cautiously for patients who experience conditions that would elevate core body temperature (e.g., strenuous exercise, ambient temperature increase, fever, or dehydration).
 
Special care and close monitoring may be necessary when administering atropine and related antimuscarinic drugs or phenobarbital to children. Neonates, infants and young children are especially sensitive to the anticholinergic effects or may experience paradoxical excitement. Certain dosage forms of Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine (e.g., Donnatal® Extentabs) may not be suitable for children.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine products are classified as FDA pregnancy risk category C. There are no adequate human studies of the effects of this drug combination upon the fetus. Therefore, in making the decision to administer this drug during pregnancy, the potential risks to the fetus must be weighed against the potential benefits to the mother. The use of phenobarbital (FDA risk category D) during pregnancy has been associated with an increased fetal risk of structural or neurobehavioral abnormalities, neonatal hemorrhage and physical dependence in the neonate. If a patient becomes pregnant or is anticipating pregnancy while receiving this drug combination, she should be warned of the potential effects.
 
Breast-feeding is not recommended while taking Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine. Phenobarbital is excreted into breast milk and can cause CNS depression in infants. There is some controversy regarding the passage of atropine and related antimuscarinic drugs into breast-milk; however, because antimuscarinic medications have been reported to inhibit lactation, some experts discourage use during breast-feeding. If the drugs are present in breast milk, then infants are usually very sensitive to anticholinergic drug effects.
 
Atropine; Hyoscyamine; Phenobarbital; Scopolamine may aggravate dental disease with chronic use because the anticholinergic effects may decrease salivary flow.
  Contraindications And Precautions
    Headline
    Drug Interactions
    General Information
    The coadministration of certain medications may lead to harm and require avoidance or therapy modification; review all drug interactions prior to concomitant use of other medications.
    Headline
    Hypersensitivity
    General Information
    This medication is contraindicated in patients with a history of hypersensitivity to it or any of its components. It is contraindicated in individuals in which phenobarbital produces restlessness or excitement.
    Headline
    Treatment-Related Restrictions of Use
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine has not been shown conclusively to be effective in the healing of duodenal ulcer, or to decrease the rate of recurrences or prevent complications.
    Headline
    substance abuse disorder
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine should be used with caution to individuals with known substance abuse disorder because of the potential for psychological and/or physical dependence to phenobarbital. Because of the potential for drug dependence and tolerance after prolonged use, avoid abrupt discontinuation.
    Headline
    activities requiring coordination and concentration, driving or operating machinery, ethanol ingestion
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine may cause blurred vision, drowsiness, or dizziness, especially with initial use. Because phenobarbital can cause drowsiness and a decreased level of consciousness, there is a higher risk of falls, particularly in the elderly, with the potential for subsequent severe injuries. Individuals should use caution when performing activities requiring coordination and concentration, such as driving or operating machinery, until they are aware of the effects of the drug. Ethanol ingestion can magnify sedative effects and is best avoided.
    Headline
    autonomic neuropathy, renal disease, urinary tract obstruction
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine is contraindicated in individuals with urinary tract obstruction. Use phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine with caution in individuals with renal disease or autonomic neuropathy.
    Headline
    geriatric
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine products should be used with caution in geriatric individuals. The elderly are generally more sensitive to the anticholinergic and sedative effects of the drugs in this product. Because phenobarbital can cause drowsiness and a decreased level of consciousness, there is a higher risk of falls, particularly in the elderly, with the potential for subsequent severe injuries. The elderly are also more likely to have medical conditions aggravated by the use of this drug combination. The effects of these drugs may be additive to other anticholinergic medications, particularly in the elderly. According to the Beers Criteria, antispasmodics such as atropine, hyoscyamine, and scopolamine are considered potentially inappropriate medications (PIMs) in geriatric individuals and should be avoided due to high anticholinergic activity and uncertain effectiveness. The Beers panel also recommends avoiding strong anticholinergic medications in geriatric individuals with the following due to the potential for symptom exacerbation or adverse effects: dementia/cognitive impairment (adverse CNS effects), delirium/high risk of delirium (new-onset or worsening delirium), or lower urinary tract symptoms/benign prostatic hyperplasia in men (urinary hesitancy or retention). In addition, barbiturates are considered PIMs in geriatric individuals and should be avoided due to the high rate of physical dependence, tolerance to sleep benefits, and risk of overdose at low dosages.
    Headline
    cardiac disease, hemorrhagic shock, hypertension, hyperthyroidism
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine should be used with caution in individuals with known cardiac disease. Antimuscarinics may alter the heart rate; the predominant clinical effect is tachycardia. Antimuscarinics should thus, be used cautiously in individuals with cardiac arrhythmias, congestive heart failure, coronary artery disease or other cardiac instability where an increase in heart rate could be detrimental. Antimuscarinics are relatively contraindicated in some individuals with acute myocardial infarction because the drug can potentiate arrhythmias. The increase in heart rate also caused by atropine increases the oxygen demand on the heart and can exacerbate myocardial ischemia. Increased heart rate is undesirable in individuals with hyperthyroidism or cardiovascular instability; use with caution in these individuals. Use in individuals with unstable cardiovascular status due to acute hemorrhagic shock is contraindicated. Antimuscarinics should be used with caution in individuals with mitral stenosis since tachycardia could exacerbate the clinical symptoms of this condition. Antimuscarinics should be used with caution in individuals with hypertension since they have some actions on the heart and can exacerbate this condition.
    Headline
    biliary tract disease, hepatic disease
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine should be used with caution in individuals with hepatic failure or hepatic disease. Use with caution in individuals with biliary tract disease; a curare-like reaction is theoretically possible with overdosage.
    Headline
    acute intermittent porphyria
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine is contraindicated in individuals with acute intermittent porphyria.
    Headline
    myasthenia gravis
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine is contraindicated in individuals with myasthenia gravis.
    Headline
    colostomy, GI obstruction, hiatal hernia, ileostomy, ulcerative colitis
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine is contraindicated in individuals with GI obstruction, including paralytic ileus and intestinal atony. Use with caution in individuals with diarrhea and an ileostomy or colostomy, because diarrhea may be an early sign of incomplete intestinal obstruction. Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine is contraindicated in severe ulcerative colitis, especially if associated with toxic megacolon. It is also contraindicated in individuals with hiatal hernia associated with reflux esophagitis.
    Headline
    glaucoma
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine is contraindicated in individuals with glaucoma.
    Headline
    conditions contributing to an elevated core body temperature
    General Information
    Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine can suppress sweat gland activity causing increases in body temperature. Use with caution in conditions contributing to an elevated core body temperature. Instruct individuals to avoid becoming overheated in hot weather, saunas, during exercise, or other strenuous activity.
    Headline
    labor, obstetric delivery, preeclampsia, pregnancy
    General Information
    The use of phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine during pregnancy may cause fetal harm. All 4 components cross the placenta. Phenobarbital carries the greatest fetal concern. Retrospective, case-controlled studies indicate an increased risk of fetal abnormalities with barbiturate exposure. Barbiturates readily cross the placenta and distribute throughout fetal tissues, with the highest concentrations found in the placenta, fetal liver, and brain. Among antiepileptic drugs, phenobarbital is associated with the highest unadjusted birth prevalence of cardiac malformations (4.4%) and one of the highest unadjusted birth prevalences of oral clefts (2.2%). Fetal cardiac screening is recommended with prenatal exposure. Barbiturates at full anesthetic doses may reduce the force and frequency of uterine contractions during labor and obstetric delivery. Use around the time of labor and delivery may also cause respiratory depression in the newborn. Neonatal coagulation defects have been reported within the first 24 hours of life following in utero phenobarbital exposure. Data on the use of scopolamine, hyoscyamine, and atropine during human pregnancy are insufficient to assess for a drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse fetal outcomes.  Scopolamine should be avoided in people with severe preeclampsia, as eclamptic seizures have been reported after exposure in this population.
    Headline
    neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero
    General Information
    Monitor neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero for signs of sedation (respiratory depression, lethargy, hypotonia), seizures, and withdrawal (hyperreflexia, irritability, restlessness, tremors, inconsolable crying, and feeding difficulties). These effects may occur at birth or be delayed up to 14 days. Phenobarbital may cause respiratory depression in the newborn when used around the time of labor and delivery. Neonatal coagulation defects have been reported within the first 24 hours of life following in utero phenobarbital exposure.
    Headline
    breast-feeding
    General Information
    Use phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine with caution during breast-feeding. All 4 components are present in milk in variable amounts. Phenobarbital is present with a relative infant dose of approximately 24% and a milk-to-plasma ratio of 0.4 to 0.6. Accumulation may occur due to phenobarbital's long half-life and immature drug elimination in the breastfed child. Monitor breastfed children for excess sedation, poor feeding, and poor weight gain, particularly younger or exclusively breastfed infants and when phenobarbital is used with other sedating medications. Withdrawal symptoms may occur if phenobarbital is discontinued or if breastfeeding is stopped. Hyoscyamine is distributed into human milk, likely in small amounts, and trace amounts of atropine have been detected in human milk. The elimination half-life of atropine is prolonged in children younger than 2 years, increasing the risk of accumulation. Scopolamine is expected to have minimal infant absorption due to poor oral bioavailability. Infants and children may be more susceptible to the anticholinergic effects of these drugs and long-term use may reduce milk production or letdown in breastfeeding individuals.

Pregnancy And Lactation
  Breastfeeding
    General Information
    Use phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine with caution during breast-feeding. All 4 components are present in milk in variable amounts. Phenobarbital is present with a relative infant dose of approximately 24% and a milk-to-plasma ratio of 0.4 to 0.6. Accumulation may occur due to phenobarbital's long half-life and immature drug elimination in the breastfed child. Monitor breastfed children for excess sedation, poor feeding, and poor weight gain, particularly younger or exclusively breastfed infants and when phenobarbital is used with other sedating medications. Withdrawal symptoms may occur if phenobarbital is discontinued or if breastfeeding is stopped. Hyoscyamine is distributed into human milk, likely in small amounts, and trace amounts of atropine have been detected in human milk. The elimination half-life of atropine is prolonged in children younger than 2 years, increasing the risk of accumulation. Scopolamine is expected to have minimal infant absorption due to poor oral bioavailability. Infants and children may be more susceptible to the anticholinergic effects of these drugs and long-term use may reduce milk production or letdown in breastfeeding individuals.

Interactions
Abacavir; Dolutegravir; lamiVUDine: (Major) Avoid concurrent use of dolutegravir with phenobarbital, as coadministration may result in decreased dolutegravir plasma concentrations. Currently, there are insufficient data to make dosing recommendations; however, predictions regarding this interaction can be made based on the drugs metabolic pathways. Phenobarbital is an inducer of CYP3A, dolutegravir is partially metabolized by this isoenzyme.
Abatacept: (Minor) Because abatacept has been shown to potentiate the onset of respiratory infections, concomitant use of drugs that decrease mucociliary clearance should be used cautiously. Anticholinergics, such as atropine, have been shown to be capable of depressing the mucociliary transport system. (Minor) Because abatacept has been shown to potentiate the onset of respiratory infections, concomitant use of drugs that decrease mucociliary clearance should be used cautiously. Anticholinergics, such as scopolamine, have been shown to be capable of depressing the mucociliary transport system.
Abemaciclib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of barbiturates with abemaciclib due to decreased exposure to abemaciclib and its active metabolites, which may lead to reduced efficacy. Abemaciclib is a CYP3A substrate and barbiturates are strong CYP3A inducers. Coadministration with another strong CYP3A inducer decreased the relative potency adjusted unbound AUC of abemaciclib plus its active metabolites (M2, M18, and M20) by approximately 70% in healthy subjects.
Abiraterone: (Major) Avoid coadministration of abiraterone with phenobarbital if possible due to decreased plasma concentrations of abiraterone. If concomitant use is unavoidable, increase the dosing frequency of abiraterone to twice daily. Reduce the dose back to the previous dose and frequency if phenobarbital is discontinued. Abiraterone is a CYP3A4 substrate and phenobarbital is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. Coadministration with another strong CYP3A4 inducer decreased abiraterone exposure by 55%.
AbobotulinumtoxinA: (Moderate) The use of systemic antimuscarinic/anticholinergic agents following the administration of botulinum toxins may result in a potentiation of systemic anticholinergic effects (e.g., blurred vision, dry mouth, constipation, or urinary retention).
Acalabrutinib: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of acalabrutinib and phenobarbital. If coadministration cannot be avoided, increase the acalabrutinib dose to 200 mg PO twice daily. Decreased acalabrutinib exposure may occur. Acalabrutinib is a CYP3A4 substrate; phenobarbital is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. In healthy subjects, the Cmax and AUC values of acalabrutinib were decreased by 68% and 77%, respectively, when acalabrutinib was coadministered with another strong CYP3A4 inducer for 9 days.
Acetaminophen: (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin, ASA; Caffeine: (Moderate) Caffeine has been reported to increase the metabolism of barbiturates, and barbiturates increase caffeine elimination. Higher caffeine doses may be needed after barbiturate administration. (Moderate) Due to high protein binding, salicylates could be displaced from binding sites, or could displace other highly protein-bound drugs such as barbiturates. An enhanced effect of the displaced drug may occur. (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin, ASA; Caffeine: (Moderate) Caffeine has been reported to increase the metabolism of barbiturates, and barbiturates increase caffeine elimination. Higher caffeine doses may be needed after barbiturate administration. (Moderate) Due to high protein binding, salicylates could be displaced from binding sites, or could displace other highly protein-bound drugs such as barbiturates. An enhanced effect of the displaced drug may occur. (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin: (Moderate) Due to high protein binding, salicylates could be displaced from binding sites, or could displace other highly protein-bound drugs such as barbiturates. An enhanced effect of the displaced drug may occur. (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin; diphenhydrAMINE: (Major) Because diphenhydramine can cause pronounced sedation, an enhanced CNS depressant effect may occur when it is combined with other CNS depressants including anxiolytics, sedatives, and hypnotics, such as barbiturates. (Moderate) Due to high protein binding, salicylates could be displaced from binding sites, or could displace other highly protein-bound drugs such as barbiturates. An enhanced effect of the displaced drug may occur. (Moderate) Monitor for signs or symptoms of anticholinergic toxicity during concomitant sedating H1-blocker and hyoscyamine use. Concomitant use may result in additive anticholinergic adverse effects. (Moderate) Monitor for unusual drowsiness or excess sedation and for signs or symptoms of anticholinergic toxicity during concomitant sedating H1-blocker and atropine use. Concomitant use may result in additive CNS depression or anticholinergic adverse effects. (Moderate) Monitor for unusual drowsiness or excess sedation and for signs or symptoms of anticholinergic toxicity during concomitant sedating H1-blocker and scopolamine use. Concomitant use may result in additive CNS depression or anticholinergic adverse effects. (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine: (Moderate) Caffeine has been reported to increase the metabolism of barbiturates, and barbiturates increase caffeine elimination. Higher caffeine doses may be needed after barbiturate administration. (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Dihydrocodeine: (Major) Concomitant use of dihydrocodeine with barbiturates may cause respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. Limit the use of opioid pain medications with barbiturates to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. It is recommended to avoid this combination when dihydrocodeine is being used for cough. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce initial dosage and titrate to clinical response; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Additionally, concomitant use of dihydrocodeine with a barbiturate can decrease dihydrocodeine concentrations, resulting in less metabolism by CYP2D6 and decreased dihydromorphine concentrations; this may result in decreased efficacy or onset of a withdrawal syndrome in patients who have developed physical dependence. Monitor for reduced efficacy of dihydrocodeine and signs of opioid withdrawal. Discontinuation of a barbiturate may increase the risk of opioid-related adverse reactions, such as fatal respiratory depression. Barbiturates induce CYP3A4, an isoenzyme partially responsible for the metabolism of dihydrocodeine. (Moderate) Caffeine has been reported to increase the metabolism of barbiturates, and barbiturates increase caffeine elimination. Higher caffeine doses may be needed after barbiturate administration. (Moderate) Monitor patients for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility when dihydrocodeine is used concomitantly with an anticholinergic drug. The concomitant use of dihydrocodeine and anticholinergic drugs may increase risk of urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. Opiates increase the tone and decrease the propulsive contractions of the smooth muscle of the gastrointestinal tract. Prolongation of the gastrointestinal transit time may be the mechanism of the constipating effect. (Minor) Chronic therapy with barbiturates can increase the metabolism and decrease the effectiveness of acetaminophen. During acute overdoses, barbiturates can enhance the formation of toxic acetaminophen metabolites.

Acetaminophen; Codeine: (Major) Concomitant use of codeine with barbiturates may cause respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. Limit the use of opioid pain medications with barbiturates to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce initial dosage and titrate to clinical response; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Additionally, concomitant use of codeine with a barbiturate can decrease codeine concentrations, resulting in less metabolism by CYP2D6 and decreased morphine concentrations; this may result in decreased efficacy or onset of a withdrawal syndrome in patients who have developed physical dependence. Monitor for reduced efficacy of codeine and signs of opioid withdrawal. Discontinuation of a barbiturate may increase the risk of opioid-related adverse reactions, such as fatal respiratory depression. Barbiturates induce CYP3A4. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A4 to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. (Major) Reserve concomitant use of codeine and atropine for patients in whom alternate treatment options are inadequate. Limit dosages and durations to the minimum required and monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation. If concomitant use is necessary, consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose and monitor for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility. Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death as well as urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. (Major) Reserve concomitant use of codeine and scopolamine for patients in whom alternate treatment options are inadequate. Limit dosages and durations to the minimum required and monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation. If concomitant use is necessary, consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose and monitor for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility. Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death as well as urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. (Moderate) Monitor for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility during concomitant codeine and hyoscyamine use. Concomitant use may increase the risk of urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. acetaZOLAMIDE: (Minor) Acetazolamide can induce osteomalacia in patients treated chronically with barbiturates. Potential mechanisms for this interaction include a carbonic anhydrase inhibitor induced increase in the urinary excretion of calcium and an increase in barbiturate effects resulting from metabolic acidosis. Acetazolamide can also increase the rate of excretion of weakly acidic drugs, such as barbiturates.
Acetylcholine Chloride: (Major) The muscarinic actions of drugs known as parasympathomimetics, including both direct cholinergic receptor agonists and cholinesterase inhibitors, can antagonize the antimuscarinic actions of anticholinergic drugs, and vice versa.
Aclidinium: (Moderate) Although aclidinium is minimally absorbed into the systemic circulation after inhalation, there is the potential for aclidinium to have additive anticholinergic effects when administered with other anticholinergics or antimuscarinics. Per the manufacturer, avoid concomitant administration of aclidinium with other anticholinergic medications, when possible.
Aclidinium; Formoterol: (Moderate) Although aclidinium is minimally absorbed into the systemic circulation after inhalation, there is the potential for aclidinium to have additive anticholinergic effects when administered with other anticholinergics or antimuscarinics. Per the manufacturer, avoid concomitant administration of aclidinium with other anticholinergic medications, when possible.
Acoramidis: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of acoramidis and barbiturates due to the risk of decreased acoramidis exposure which may reduce its efficacy.
Adagrasib: (Major) Avoid concurrent use of adagrasib and barbiturates due to the risk of decreased adagrasib exposure which may reduce its efficacy. Adagrasib is a CYP3A substrate and barbiturates is a strong CYP3A inducer. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inducer reduced adagrasib exposure by more than 66%.
Afatinib: (Major) Increase the daily dose of afatinib by 10 mg as tolerated if the concomitant use with phenobarbital is necessary; resume the previous dose of afatinib 2 to 3 days after discontinuation of phenobarbital. Afatinib is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and phenobarbital is a P-gp inducer; coadministration may decrease plasma concentrations of afatinib. Pre-treatment with another strong P-gp inducer decreased afatinib exposure by 34%.
Aficamten: (Moderate) Monitor for a decrease in aficamten efficacy during concomitant use of aficamten and barbiturates; increase the aficamten dosage as appropriate based on response. If a barbiturate is discontinued, reduce the aficamten dosage by approximately half: 20 mg to 10 mg, 15 mg to 5 mg, 10 mg to 5 mg, or continue 5 mg. Concomitant use may decrease aficamten exposure. Aficamten is a CYP3A substrate and barbiturates are strong CYP3A inducers. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inducer reduced aficamten overall exposure by 51%.
Albendazole: (Minor) Phenobarbital appears to induce the oxidative metabolism of albendazole. Notably, a significant reduction in the plasma concentration of the active albendazole sulfoxide metabolite may occur. It is not clear if these pharmacokinetic interactions affect the therapeutic efficacy of albendazole in the treatment of neurocysticercosis. Monitor patient clinical response closely during treatment.
Albuterol; Budesonide: (Moderate) Coadministration may result in decreased exposure to budesonide. Phenobarbital is a CYP3A4 inducer; budesonide is a CYP3A4 substrate. Monitor for decreased response to budesonide during concurrent use. Dose adjustments may be necessary.
ALFentanil: (Major) Concomitant use of alfentanil with a barbiturate may cause respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. Limit the use of opioid pain medications with a barbiturate to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce initial dosage and titrate to clinical response; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Additionally, concurrent use of alfentanil with a barbiturate may decrease alfentanil plasma concentrations, decrease opioid efficacy, and potentially lead to a withdrawal syndrome in those with physical dependence to opioid agonists. Monitor for signs of opioid withdrawal. Discontinuation of a barbiturate may increase the risk of increased opioid-related adverse reactions, such as fatal respiratory depression. Barbiturates induce CYP3A4; alfentanil is a CYP3A4 substrate. (Moderate) Monitor patients for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility when alfentanil is used concomitantly with an anticholinergic drug. The concomitant use of alfentanil and anticholinergic drugs may increase risk of urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. Opiates increase the tone and decrease the propulsive contractions of the smooth muscle of the gastrointestinal tract. Prolongation of the gastrointestinal transit time may be the mechanism of the constipating effect.
Aliskiren; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Barbiturates may potentiate orthostatic hypotension when used concurrently with thiazide diuretics. (Minor) Coadministration of thiazides and antimuscarinics (e.g., atropine and biperiden) may result in increased bioavailability of the thiazide. This is apparently a result of a decrease in gastrointestinal motility and rate of stomach emptying by the antimuscarinic agent. In addition, diuretics can increase urinary frequency, which may aggravate bladder symptoms.
Alogliptin; Pioglitazone: (Minor) It is possible that a decrease in exposure of pioglitazone will occur when coadministered with drugs that induce CYP2C8 including phenobarbital. Patients receiving phenobarbital in combination with pioglitazone should be monitored for changes in glycemic control; dosage adjustments may be necessary.
Alosetron: (Major) Concomitant use of alosetron and anticholinergics, which can decrease GI motility, may seriously worsen constipation, leading to events such as GI obstuction, impaction, or paralytic ileus. Although specific recommendations are not available from the manufacturer, it would be prudent to avoid anticholinergics in patients taking alosetron. (Minor) Alosetron is metabolized by CYP1A2 and CYP3A4. Phenobarbital can induce the activity of these enzymes and increase the metabolism of alosetron. Concomitant administration of phenobarbital and alosetron has not been evaluated.
Alpelisib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of alpelisib with phenobarbital due to decreased exposure to alpelisib which could decrease efficacy. Alpelisib is a CYP3A4 substrate; phenobarbital is a strong CYP3A4 inducer.
ALPRAZolam: (Moderate) Monitor for reduced efficacy of alprazolam and signs of benzodiazepine withdrawal if coadministration with barbiturates is necessary. Alprazolam is a CYP3A4 substrate and barbiturates are strong CYP3A4 inducers. Concomitant use with CYP3A4 inducers can decrease alprazolam concentrations; this may result in decreased efficacy or onset of a withdrawal syndrome in patients who have developed physical dependence. Additionally, monitor for excessive sedation and somnolence during coadministration of alprazolam and barbiturates. Concurrent use may result in additive CNS depression. (Moderate) Scopolamine may cause dizziness and drowsiness. Concurrent use of scopolamine and CNS depressants can adversely increase the risk of CNS depression.
Aluminum Hydroxide: (Moderate) Antacids may inhibit the oral absorption of anticholinergics. Simultaneous oral administration should be avoided when feasible; separate dosing by at least 2 hours to limit an interaction.
Aluminum Hydroxide; Magnesium Carbonate: (Moderate) Antacids may inhibit the oral absorption of anticholinergics. Simultaneous oral administration should be avoided when feasible; separate dosing by at least 2 hours to limit an interaction.

Aluminum Hydroxide; Magnesium Hydroxide; Simethicone: (Moderate) Antacids may inhibit the oral absorption of anticholinergics. Simultaneous oral administration should be avoided when feasible; separate dosing by at least 2 hours to limit an interaction.
Aluminum Hydroxide; Magnesium Trisilicate: (Moderate) Antacids may inhibit the oral absorption of anticholinergics. Simultaneous oral administration should be avoided when feasible; separate dosing by at least 2 hours to limit an interaction.
Amantadine: (Major) Additive anticholinergic effects may be seen when hyoscyamine is used concomitantly with other drugs that possess antimuscarinic effects. (Major) Additive anticholinergic effects may be seen when scopolamine is used concomitantly with other antimuscarinics, such as amantadine. Clinicians should note that antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. (Major) Amantadine may exhibit anticholinergic activity. Antimuscarinics, such as atropine, may potentiate the anticholinergic effects of amantadine, and may increase the risk of antimuscarinic-related side effects.
Amikacin: (Minor) Antiemetics, like scopolamine, should be used carefully with amikacin because they can mask symptoms of ototoxicity (e.g., nausea secondary to vertigo). These agents block the histamine or acetylcholine response that causes nausea due to vestibular (inner ear) emetic stimuli such as motion.

(Minor) Coadministration of thiazides and antimuscarinics (e.g., atropine and biperiden) may result in increased bioavailability of the thiazide.
