Description
  Text
  NOTE: This monograph discusses the use of phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine combination products to treat irritable bowel syndrome. Clinicians may wish to consult the individual monographs for more information about each agent.
Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine are used together in an oral preparation to treat GI-related problems such as irritable bowel syndrome. Atropine, hyoscyamine, and scopolamine are the active components of belladonna and are sometimes referred to as belladonna alkaloids or belladonna extract. Atropine, hyoscyamine, and scopolamine are anticholinergic agents and are used in this combination product to decrease GI motility and for their antisecretory effects. Phenobarbital is a barbiturate agent used for its sedative actions. Historically, this combination was one of the first drugs used in the treatment of duodenal ulcer, however, more effective agents (e.g., H2-blockers, Gastric acid-pump inhibitors) with less potential for adverse effects are available. Conclusive evidence supporting whether anticholinergic/antispasmodic drugs aid in the healing of a duodenal ulcer, decrease the rate of ulcer recurrences, or prevent complications is not available. Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine has been available since before 1938.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Phenobarbital: The sedative-hypnotic effects of phenobarbital are believed to be a result of its effect in the polysynaptic midbrain reticular formation, which controls CNS arousal. Phenobarbital also causes a non-selective depressant effect throughout the CNS, with a slow onset but a long duration of action. Sedative effects last for several hours, but other CNS effects, such as distorted judgment and impaired motor skills, can last for several days.
Hyoscyamine, Atropine, Scopolamine: These compounds, the active alkaloids found in belladonna, are competitive inhibitors at autonomic postganglionic cholinergic receptors. These include receptors found in GI and pulmonary smooth muscle, exocrine glands, the heart, and the eye. They do not block the actions of acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction. The degree of sensitivity of various muscarinic receptors to antimuscarinic agents is dose-dependent. The most sensitive receptors are those of the salivary, bronchial, and sweat glands. Next are the receptors in the eye and heart, followed by the receptors in the GI tract. The principal clinical effects of antimuscarinics are a reduction in salivary, bronchial, and sweat gland secretions; mydriasis; cycloplegia; changes in heart rate; contraction of the bladder detrusor muscle and of the GI smooth muscle; decreased gastric secretion; and decreased GI motility. At lower doses, a paradoxical decrease in heart rate occurs. At higher doses, effects are seen at nicotinic receptors in autonomic ganglia, causing restlessness, hallucinations, disorientation, and/or delirium. All three agents cross the blood-brain barrier, but scopolamine is the only one that causes CNS depression (drowsiness, euphoria, amnesia, fatigue, decreased REM sleep) at usual therapeutic doses.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Phenobarbital: Following oral administration, roughly 70% to 90% of the dose of phenobarbital is absorbed from the gut. Absorption is delayed by the presence of food. Peak serum concentrations are usually achieved 8 to 12 hours after oral dosing.
Hyoscyamine: Hyoscyamine is well absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract. Food does not affect absorption. Peak pharmacologic effects occur within 30 to 60 minutes and lasts for about 4 hours.
Atropine: Atropine is well absorbed after oral administration. Peak plasma concentrations are seen within 1 hour after oral administration.
Scopolamine: Scopolamine is well absorbed from the GI tract. Effects occur 15 to 30 minutes after oral administration.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Phenobarbital: Phenobarbital may accumulate in patients with renal failure or severe renal impairment.
Hyoscyamine: The elimination half-life of hyoscyamine is prolonged in patients with impaired renal function.
  Text
  Phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine combination products are administered orally.
Phenobarbital: Phenobarbital is the longest acting of all the commercially available barbiturates. It may take several weeks of dosing to reach steady-state due to the long half-life of the drug. Therapeutic plasma levels (for anticonvulsant activity) are roughly 10 to 40 mcg/mL. Plasma levels greater than 50 mcg/mL may produce coma and levels greater than 80 mcg/mL are potentially fatal. Distribution of phenobarbital is less rapid than that of other barbiturates because it is less lipid-soluble, but the drug is found in all tissues and fluids, including adipose tissue and cerebrospinal fluid. Only unionized drug crosses the blood brain barrier; acidosis can increase the pharmacologic effect of phenobarbital by increasing the concentration of unionized drug. About 20% to 45% of phenobarbital is bound to plasma proteins. The half-life of phenobarbital is long and in adults ranges from 50 to 120 hours (mean: 96 hours). Elimination of unchanged phenobarbital in urine is about 25%, but the excretion is pH-dependent. Increasing the urinary flow rate or alkalinizing the urine will increase the rate of excretion of unchanged phenobarbital. The remainder of the dose (roughly 75%) is inactivated in the liver, primarily via CYP2C9, with minor metabolism by CYP2C19 and CYP2E1. Phenobarbital accelerates the clearance of other drugs metabolized via hepatic microsomal enzymes (e.g., UGT enzymes, CYP2C-family enzymes, CYP3A-family enzymes and CYP1A2), but there is no clear evidence that phenobarbital accelerates its own metabolism. The metabolites of phenobarbital are excreted as the p-hydroxy metabolite, the glucuronide, and sulfate conjugates.
Hyoscyamine: Once in the systemic circulation, hyoscyamine is well distributed throughout the body. The drug crosses the blood-brain barrier and small amounts distribute into milk and can be found in placental tissues. Protein binding is about 50%. Hyoscyamine is metabolized in the liver to tropic acid, tropine, and hyoscyamine glucuronide. Excretion occurs in the urine primarily as unchanged drug (approximately 30 to 50%) and metabolites. The elimination half-life in patients with normal renal function is about 3.5 hours.
Atropine: After absorption, the drug is widely distributed throughout the body and crosses the blood-brain barrier and the placenta. It is metabolized in the liver to several metabolites including tropic acid. Atropine's initial half-life is about 2 to 3 hours, and its terminal half-life is about 12.5 hours. Atropine and metabolites are primarily excreted renally and, to a lesser extent, by the pulmonary and fecal routes.
Scopolamine: After absorption, scopolamine is distributed throughout the body and crosses the placenta and the blood-brain barrier. The metabolism of scopolamine is not completely known, but the drug is believed to be almost completely metabolized in the liver and excreted renally as metabolites. The elimination half-life is about 8 hours.

Maximum Dosage Limits
  Text
  No maximum dosage information is available. Do not exceed manufacturer dosing recommendations for any age group.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Initial doses should be at the lower end of the dosage range; however, no quantitative dosage adjustment recommendations are available for phenobarbital; hyoscyamine; atropine; scopolamine.
  Renal Impairment
  Specific guidelines for dosage adjustments in renal impairment are not available. The chronic use of phenobarbital should generally be avoided in patients with renal failure.
